\displaystyle\phi:\mathcal{A}\ni x\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}u^{*}xu&u^{*}xv\\
v^{*}xu&v^{*}xv\end{pmatrix}\in M_{2}(\mathcal{A})

(6.6.4)

is a C*-algebra isomorphism with the inverse map

\displaystyle\psi:M_{2}(\mathcal{A})\ni\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\mapsto uau^{*}+ubv^{*}+vcu^{*}+vdv^{*}\in\mathcal{A}.

(6.6.5)

Proof.

Using Equation (
6.6.3
), a direct computation gives

\displaystyle\phi\psi\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\phi(uau^{*}+ubv^{*}+vcu^{*}+vdv^{*})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}u^{*}(uau^{*}+ubv^{*}+vcu^{*}+vdv^{*})u&u^{*}(uau^{*}+ubv^{*}+vcu^{*}+vdv^{*})v\\
v^{*}(uau^{*}+ubv^{*}+vcu^{*}+vdv^{*})u&v^{*}(uau^{*}+ubv^{*}+vcu^{*}+vdv^{*})v\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1_{\mathcal{A}}a1_{\mathcal{A}}+1_{\mathcal{A}}b0+0c1_{\mathcal{A}}+0d0&1_{\mathcal{A}}a0+1_{\mathcal{A}}b1_{\mathcal{A}}+0c0+0d1_{\mathcal{A}}\\
0a1_{\mathcal{A}}+0b0+1_{\mathcal{A}}c1_{\mathcal{A}}+1_{\mathcal{A}}d0&0a0+0b1_{\mathcal{A}}+1_{\mathcal{A}}c0+1_{\mathcal{A}}d1_{\mathcal{A}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix},\quad\forall\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in M_{2}(\mathcal{A})

and

\displaystyle\psi\phi x

\displaystyle=\psi\begin{pmatrix}u^{*}xu&u^{*}xv\\
v^{*}xu&v^{*}xv\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=u(u^{*}xu)u^{*}+u(u^{*}xv)v^{*}+v(v^{*}xu)u^{*}+v(v^{*}xv)v^{*}

\displaystyle=uu^{*}x(uu^{*}+vv^{*})+vv^{*}x(uu^{*}+vv^{*})

\displaystyle=uu^{*}x1_{\mathcal{A}}+vv^{*}x1_{\mathcal{A}}=(uu^{*}+vv^{*})x=x,\quad\forall x\in\mathcal{A}.

Further,

\displaystyle(\phi(x))^{*}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}u^{*}xu&u^{*}xv\\
v^{*}xu&v^{*}xv\end{pmatrix}^{*}=\begin{pmatrix}u^{*}x^{*}u&(v^{*}xu)^{*}\\
(u^{*}xv)^{*}&v^{*}x^{*}v\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}u^{*}x^{*}u&u^{*}x^{*}v\\
v^{*}x^{*}u&v^{*}x^{*}v\end{pmatrix}=\phi(x^{*}),\quad\forall x\in\mathcal{A}.

Hence
\phi
is a *-isomorphism. Using Theorem
6.6.3
, to show
\phi
is a C*-algebra isomorphism (i.e., isometric isomorphism), it suffices to show that
\phi
is injective. Let
x\in\mathcal{A}
be such that
\phi x=0
. Then

\displaystyle u^{*}xu=u^{*}xv=0,\quad v^{*}xv=v^{*}xu=0.

Using the first equation we get
uu^{*}xuu^{*}=uu^{*}xvv^{*}=0
which implies
uu^{*}x=uu^{*}x(uu^{*}+vv^{*})=0
. Similarly using the second equation we get
vv^{*}x=0
. Therefore
x=(uu^{*}+vv^{*})x=0
. Hence
\phi
is injective which completes the proof.
∎

Along with the lines of Theorem
6.6.4
we can easily derive the following result.

Theorem 6.6.5
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a unital C*-algebra and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. If there are isometries
u,v\in\mathcal{A}
such that Equation (
6.6.3
) holds, then the map

\displaystyle\phi:M_{n}(\mathcal{A})\ni X\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}u^{*}Xu&u^{*}Xv\\
v^{*}Xu&v^{*}Xv\end{pmatrix}\in M_{2n}(\mathcal{A})

is a C*-algebra isomorphism with the inverse map

\displaystyle\psi:M_{2n}(\mathcal{A})\ni\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}\mapsto uAu^{*}+uBv^{*}+vCu^{*}+vDv^{*}\in M_{n}(\mathcal{A}),

where if
X\coloneqq[x_{i,j}]_{i,j}
is a matrix, and
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
, by
aXb
we mean the matrix
[ax_{i,j}b]_{i,j}
. In particular, the C*-algebras
\mathcal{A},M_{2}(\mathcal{A}),M_{4}(\mathcal{A}),\dots,M_{2n}(\mathcal{A}),\dots
are all *-isometrically isomorphic.

In the rest of this chapter, we assume that unital C*-algebra
\mathcal{A}
has isometries
u,v
satisfying Equation (
6.6.3
). In the next result we use the following notation. Given a vector
x\in\mathcal{A}^{n}
,
x_{i}
means its
i^{\text{th}}
coordinate.

Proposition 6.6.6
.

Let
n\geq 2
and
T:\mathcal{A}^{n}\to\mathcal{A}^{n-1}
be the bounded linear operator defined by

\displaystyle T(b_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\coloneqq([v,b_{i}]+[u,b_{i-1}])_{i=2}^{n},\quad\forall(b_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\in\mathcal{A}^{n}.

Then there exists a bounded linear right-inverse
R:\mathcal{A}^{n-1}\to\mathcal{A}^{n}
for
T
such that

\displaystyle\|Rb\|

\displaystyle=\sup_{1\leq i\leq n}\|(Rb)_{i}\|\leq 8\sqrt{2}n^{2}\sup_{2\leq i\leq n}\|b_{i}\|

\displaystyle\leq 8\sqrt{2}n^{2}\sup_{1\leq i\leq n}\|b_{i}\|=8\sqrt{2}n^{2}\|b\|,\quad\forall b\in\mathcal{A}^{n}.

Proof.

Define

\displaystyle L:\mathcal{A}^{n-1}\ni(x_{i})_{i=2}^{n}\mapsto\left(-\frac{1}{2}x_{i}v^{*}-\frac{1}{2}x_{i+1}u^{*}\right)_{i=1}^{n}\in\mathcal{A}^{n},\quad\text{ where }x_{1}\coloneqq 0,x_{n+1}\coloneqq 0

and

\displaystyle E:\mathcal{A}^{n-1}\ni(x_{i})_{i=2}^{n}\mapsto\left(\frac{1}{2}(vx_{i}v^{*}+vx_{i+1}u^{*}+ux_{i-1}v^{*}+ux_{i}u^{*})\right)_{i=2}^{n}\in\mathcal{A}^{n-1}.

Then

\displaystyle TL(x_{i})_{i=2}^{n}=T\left(-\frac{1}{2}x_{i}v^{*}-\frac{1}{2}x_{i+1}u^{*}\right)_{i=1}^{n}=-\frac{1}{2}(T(x_{i}v^{*})_{i=2}^{n}+T(x_{i+1}u^{*})_{i=2}^{n})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}(([v,x_{i}v^{*}]+[u,x_{i-1}v^{*}])_{i=2}^{n}+([v,x_{i+1}u^{*}]+[u,x_{i}u^{*}])_{i=2}^{n})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}(vx_{i}v^{*}-x_{i}v^{*}v+ux_{i-1}v^{*}-x_{i-1}v^{*}u+vx_{i+1}u^{*}-x_{i+1}u^{*}v+ux_{i}u^{*}-x_{i}u^{*}u)_{i=2}^{n}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}(vx_{i}v^{*}-x_{i}+ux_{i-1}v^{*}-x_{i-1}v^{*}u+vx_{i+1}u^{*}+ux_{i}u^{*}-x_{i})_{i=2}^{n}

\displaystyle=(x_{i})_{i=2}^{n}-\frac{1}{2}(vx_{i}v^{*}+vx_{i+1}u^{*}+ux_{i-1}v^{*}+ux_{i}u^{*})_{i=1}^{n}

\displaystyle=(1-E)(x_{i})_{i=2}^{n},\quad\forall(x_{i})_{i=2}^{n}\in\mathcal{A}^{n-1},\quad\text{ where }1(x_{i})_{i=2}^{n}\coloneqq(x_{i})_{i=2}^{n}.

We next try to show that the operator
1-E
is bounded invertible with the help of Neumann series. First step is to change the norm on
\mathcal{A}^{n}
to an equivalent norm so that invertibility property will not affect in both norms. Define a new norm on
\mathcal{A}^{n-1}
by

\displaystyle\|(x_{i})_{i=2}^{n}\|^{\prime}\coloneqq\sup_{2\leq i\leq n}\left(2-\frac{i^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)^{\frac{-1}{2}}\|x_{i}\|.

Let
x=(x_{i})_{i=2}^{n}\in\mathcal{A}^{n-1}
be such that
\|(x_{i})_{i=2}^{n}\|^{\prime}\leq 1
. Then

\displaystyle\left(2-\frac{i^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)^{\frac{-1}{2}}\|x_{i}\|\leq\sup_{2\leq i\leq n}\left(2-\frac{i^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)^{\frac{-1}{2}}\|x_{i}\|\leq 1,\quad\forall 2\leq i\leq n.

Hence
\|x_{i}\|\leq\left(2-\frac{i^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
for all
2\leq i\leq n
. Using Theorem
6.6.4
we now get

\displaystyle\|(Ex)_{i}\|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|vx_{i}v^{*}+vx_{i+1}u^{*}+ux_{i-1}v^{*}+ux_{i}u^{*}\|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\|\begin{pmatrix}x_{i}&x_{i+1}\\
x_{i-1}&x_{i}\end{pmatrix}\right\|\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\|\begin{pmatrix}\|x_{i}\|&\|x_{i+1}\|\\
\|x_{i-1}\|&\|x_{i}\|\end{pmatrix}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\|x_{i}\|^{2}+\|x_{i+1}\|^{2}+\|x_{i-1}\|^{2}+\|x_{i}\|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\left(2-\frac{i^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)+\left(2-\frac{(i+1)^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)+\left(2-\frac{(i-1)^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)+\left(2-\frac{i^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(2-\frac{i^{2}}{n^{2}}-\frac{1}{2n^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{8n^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(2-\frac{i^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{8n^{2}}\right)\left(2-\frac{i^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad\forall 2\leq i\leq n.

Hence

\displaystyle\|Ex\|^{\prime}=\sup_{2\leq i\leq n}\left(2-\frac{i^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)^{\frac{-1}{2}}\|(Ex)_{i}\|\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{8n^{2}}\right)\|x\|^{\prime},\quad\forall x\in\mathcal{A}^{n-1}.

Since
1-\frac{1}{8n^{2}}<1
,
1-E
is invertible and
\|(1-E)^{-1}x\|^{\prime}\leq 8n^{2}\|x\|^{\prime}
. Now going back to the original norm, we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\|((1-E)^{-1}x)_{i}\|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{2\leq i\leq n}\left(2-\frac{i^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)^{\frac{-1}{2}}\|((1-E)^{-1}x)_{i}\|

\displaystyle=\|(1-E)^{-1}x\|^{\prime}\leq 8n^{2}\|x\|^{\prime}

\displaystyle=8n^{2}\sup_{2\leq i\leq n}\left(2-\frac{i^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)^{\frac{-1}{2}}\|x_{i}\|

\displaystyle\leq 8n^{2}\sup_{2\leq i\leq n}\|x_{i}\|=8n^{2}\|x\|,\quad\forall x\in\mathcal{A}^{n-1}.

Define
R\coloneqq L(1-E)^{-1}
. Then
TR=TL(1-E)^{-1}=(1-E)(1-E)^{-1}=1
and

\displaystyle\|Rb\|

\displaystyle=\sup_{1\leq i\leq n}\|(Rb)_{i}\|=\|L(1-E)^{-1}b\|\leq\|L\|\|(1-E)^{-1}b\|\leq\|(1-E)^{-1}b\|

\displaystyle=\sup_{2\leq i\leq n}\|((1-E)^{-1}b)_{i}\|\leq 8\sqrt{2}n^{2}\|b\|=8\sqrt{2}n^{2}\sup_{2\leq i\leq n}\|b_{i}\|,\quad\forall b\in\mathcal{A}^{n-1}.

∎

As given in
Tao, 2019
we try to shift from the systems of equations (
6.6.1
) and (
6.6.2
) to the solution of single equation. Let
n\geq 2
. Define
a\coloneqq(0,\dots,n)\in\mathcal{A}^{n}
,

\displaystyle F:\mathcal{A}^{n}\ni(b_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\mapsto(-2b_{3},\dots,-(n-1)b_{n},0)\in\mathcal{A}^{n-1}

and

\displaystyle G:\mathcal{A}^{n}\times\mathcal{A}^{n}\ni((b_{i})_{i=1}^{n},(c_{i})_{i=1}^{n})\mapsto(-b_{2}[u,c_{n}],\dots,-b_{n}[u,c_{n}])\in\mathcal{A}^{n-1}.

We then have
\|F\|\leq n-1
and
\|G\|\leq 2
.

Proposition 6.6.7
.

Systems (
6.6.1
) and (
6.6.2
) have a solution
b
if and only if

\displaystyle Tb=a+\delta F(b)+\delta G(b,b).

(6.6.6)

Proof.

Systems (
6.6.1
) and (
6.6.2
) have a solution
b
if and only if

\displaystyle[v,b_{i}]+[u,b_{i-1}]=-i\delta b_{i+1}-\delta b_{i}[u,b_{n}],\quad\forall i=2,\dots,n-1

and

\displaystyle[v,b_{n}]+[u,b_{n-1}]=-\delta b_{n}[u,b_{n}]+n\cdot 1_{{M_{n}(\mathcal{A})}}

if and only if

\displaystyle([v,b_{i}]+[u,b_{i-1}])_{i=2}^{n}=

\displaystyle\quad(0,\dots,n)+\delta(-2b_{3},\dots,-(n-1)b_{n},0)+\delta(-b_{2}[u,b_{n}],\dots,-b_{n}[u,b_{n}])

if and only if

\displaystyle Tb=a+\delta F(b)+\delta G(b,b).

∎

The above proposition reduces the work of solving systems (
6.6.1
) and (
6.6.2
) to a single operator equation. To solve (
6.6.6
) we need an abstract lemma from
Tao, 2019
.

Lemma 6.6.8
.

(
Tao, 2019
)
Let
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{Y}
be Banach spaces,
T,F:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
be bounded linear operators, and let

G:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
be a bounded bilinear operator with bound
r>0
and let
a\in\mathcal{Y}
. Suppose that

T
has a bounded linear right inverse
R:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}
. If
\delta>0
is such that

\displaystyle\delta(2\|F\|\|R\|+4r\|R\|^{2}\|a\|)<1,

(6.6.7)

then there exists
b\in\mathcal{X}
with
\|b\|\leq 2\|R\|\|a\|
that solves the equation

\displaystyle Tb=a+\delta F(b)+\delta G(b,b).

Theorem 6.6.9
.

For each
n\geq 2
, there exists a solution
b
to Equation (
6.6.6
) such that
\|b\|\leq 16\sqrt{2}n^{3}
.

Proof.

We apply Lemma
6.6.8
for

\displaystyle\delta\coloneqq\frac{1}{2000n^{5}}.

Then using Proposition
6.6.6
, we get

\displaystyle\delta(2\|F\|\|R\|+4r\|R\|^{2}\|a\|)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2000n^{5}}(2(n-1)8\sqrt{2}n^{2}+4.2.128.n^{4}.n)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2000n^{5}}(16\sqrt{2}n^{3}+1024n^{5})<1.

Lemma
6.6.8
now says that there exists a
b
which satisfies (
6.6.6
).
∎

Theorem 6.6.10
.

For each
n\geq 2
, let
b
be an element satisfying Equation (
6.6.6
) and
\|b\|\leq 16\sqrt{2}n^{3}
. Then for
\mu=\frac{1}{2}
,
D_{\mu},X_{\mu}\in M_{n}(\mathcal{A})
such that

\displaystyle\|D_{\mu}\|=O(n^{5}),\quad\|X_{\mu}\|=O(1),\quad\|[D_{\mu},X_{\mu}]-1_{M_{n}(\mathcal{A})}\|=O(n^{3}2^{-n}).

Proof.

Let
D_{\mu},X_{\mu}\in M_{n}(\mathcal{A})
be as in Corollary
6.6.2
. We then have

\displaystyle\|D_{\mu}\|\leq 4.2000n^{5}\|u\|+2.2000n^{5}\|v\|+(n-1)+\frac{1}{2000n^{5}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{2^{n-i-1}}16\sqrt{2}n^{3}\|u\|

\displaystyle\quad\quad=O(n^{5}),

\displaystyle\|X_{\mu}\|\leq 1+\frac{1}{2000n^{5}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{2^{n-i-1}}16\sqrt{2}n^{3}=O(1),

\displaystyle\|[D_{\mu},X_{\mu}]-1_{M_{n}(\mathcal{A})}\|\leq 2\mu^{n-1}(\|v\|\|b_{1}\|+\delta\|b_{2}\|+\delta\|b_{1}\|\|u\|\|b_{n}\|).

\displaystyle\quad\leq 2\frac{1}{2^{n-1}}(\|u\|16\sqrt{2}n^{3}+\frac{1}{2000n^{5}}16\sqrt{2}n^{3}+\frac{1}{2000n^{5}}16\sqrt{2}n^{3}\|u\|16\sqrt{2}n^{3})

\displaystyle\quad\leq 2\frac{n^{3}}{2^{n-1}}(\|v\|16\sqrt{2}+\frac{1}{2000n^{5}}16\sqrt{2}+\frac{1}{2000n^{5}}16\sqrt{2}n^{3}\|u\|16\sqrt{2})=O(n^{3}2^{-n}).

∎

Theorem 6.6.11
.

Let
0<\varepsilon\leq 1/2
. Then there exist an even integer
n
and
D,X\in M_{n}(\mathcal{A})
with

\displaystyle\|[D,X]-1_{M_{n}(\mathcal{A})}\|\leq\varepsilon

such that

\displaystyle\|D\|\|X\|=O\left(\log^{5}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right).

Proof.

Let
D_{\mu},X_{\mu}\in M_{n}(\mathcal{A})
be as in Corollary
6.6.2
. Theorem
6.6.10
says that there are
\alpha,\beta,\gamma>0
be such that

\displaystyle\|D_{\mu}\|\leq\alpha n^{5},\quad\|X_{\mu}\|\leq\beta,\quad\|[D_{\mu},X_{\mu}]-1_{M_{n}(\mathcal{A})}\|\leq\gamma n^{3}2^{-n}.

Since
2^{n}>n^{4}
all but finitely many
n
’s,
\gamma n^{3}2^{-n}<\varepsilon
all but finitely many
n
’s.
We now choose real
c
such that
n=c\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
is even
\gamma n^{3}2^{-n}<\varepsilon
. We then have
\|D_{\mu}\|=O(\log^{5}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
and
\|[D_{\mu},X_{\mu}]-1_{M_{n}(\mathcal{A})}\|\leq\varepsilon
.
∎

Theorem
6.6.11
and Theorem
6.6.5
easily give the following.

Theorem 6.6.12
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a unital C*-algebra. Suppose there are isometries
u,v\in\mathcal{A}
such that Equation (
6.6.3
) holds. Then
for each
0<\varepsilon\leq 1/2
, there exist
d,x\in\mathcal{A}
with

\displaystyle\|[d,x]-1_{\mathcal{A}}\|\leq\varepsilon

such that

\displaystyle\|d\|\|x\|=O\left(\log^{5}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right).

Remark 6.6.13
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a finite dimensional unital C*-algebra. From the structure theory (
Davidson, 1996
) we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\cong M_{n_{1}}(\mathbb{C})\oplus\cdots\oplus M_{n_{r}}(\mathbb{C}),

for unique (upto permutation) natural numbers
n_{1},\dots,n_{r}
. This result says that normalized trace map (a trace map
Tr
such that
\text{Tr}(1_{\mathcal{A}})=1
) exists on
\mathcal{A}
. Using this we make the following two observations.

(i)

\mathcal{A}
can not have isometries satisfying Equation (
6.6.3
). Suppose that there are such isometries. Then

\displaystyle 1=\text{Tr}(uu^{*}+vv^{*})=\text{Tr}(uu^{*})+\text{Tr}(vv^{*})=\text{Tr}(u^{*}u)+\text{Tr}(v^{*}v)=2

which is impossible.

(ii)

In (
Tao, 2019
), Tao observed that if
\mathcal{H}
is a finite dimensional Hilbert space, then there are no
D,X\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
satisfying
\|[D,X]-1_{\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})}\|<1
. We elaborate this for any finite dimensional unital C*-algebra
\mathcal{A}
, namely, there do not exist
d,x\in\mathcal{A}

satisfying
\|[d,x]-1_{\mathcal{A}}\|<1
. In other words, Theorem
6.6.12
fails for every finite dimensional unital C*-algebra. Let
d,x\in\mathcal{A}
be arbitrary. From the structure theory, we identify that
d
as
D
and
x
as
X
for some matrices
D,X\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
and for some
n
. Using the commutativity of trace we then have
\text{Tr}([D,X])=0
. Let
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}
be eigenvalues of
[D,X]
. Then
\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}=\text{Tr}([D,X])=0
. This gives

\displaystyle n=\left|\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\lambda_{j}-1)\right|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}|\lambda_{j}-1|.

Previous inequality says that there is atleast one
j
such that
|\lambda_{j}-1|\geq 1
. We next see that all the eigenvalues of

[D,X]-1_{M_{n}(\mathbb{C})}
are
\lambda_{1}-1,\dots,\lambda_{n}-1
. Using the property of operator norm we finally get

\displaystyle\|[d,x]-1_{\mathcal{A}}\|=\|[D,X]-1_{M_{n}(\mathbb{C})}\|\geq\sup_{1\leq j\leq n}|\lambda_{j}-1|\geq 1.

Conclusion and future work :

In this appendix we showed that the result of Tao’s is valid in more general spaces. One of the future objectives is to improve the bounds in Theorem
6.6.12
and Theorem
6.5.8
.
